### 2.3.5. Dynamique d’évolution des pratiques agricoles suite à l’introduction de la méthanisation

En fonction des réponses des agriculteurs et de notre analyse quantitative précédente, il est apparu que les changements de pratiques induits par l’introduction de la méthanisation suivent des dynamiques complexes et variées. Les données recueillies lors de nos enquêtes montrent que les réponses des agriculteurs sont influencées par une multitude de facteurs locaux, économiques et techniques. A partir de ces observations, nous avons tenté d’identifier les différentes logiques d’évolution qui caractérisent la manière dont les exploitations ont adapté leur système à la présence d’unité de méthanisation. Ces logiques se basent sur des facteurs spécifiques, liés à la transformation des systèmes de production la diversification des activités, l’intégration de nouvelles technologies, ou encore l’optimisation des pratiques existantes. Pour réaliser cette partie, nous avons structuré notre analyse autour de cinq indicateurs caractérisant chaque exploitation qui nous semblaient impactants dans les dynamiques et logiques d’insertion de la méthanisation dans les exploitations agricoles au vu de l’analyse quantitative présentées ci-dessus. Ces indicateurs permettent de synthétiser des informations clés sur l’évolution des pratiques et des productions dans les exploitations. Ces cinq indicateurs sont les suivants : 1. Proportion de la SAU en CIVE : Cet indicateur a été calculé à partir des données quantitatives fournies par les exploitants. Il reflète la part de la Surface Agricole Utile consacrée aux CIVE, un aspect essentiel pour comprendre les ajustements dans les systèmes de culture. [SOURCE: Page_94]

94 [SOURCE: Page_94]

Chapitre 2 [SOURCE: Page_95]

2.   Proportion massique d’intrants extérieurs dans la ration totale : De manière similaire, cet indicateur mesure la part des intrants extérieurs (hors exploitation) dans l’alimentation de l’unité de méthanisation. Ce calcul a également été basé sur les données quantitatives fournies par les exploitants, permettant de quantifier la dépendance aux intrants externes. 3. Proportion d’effluent d’élevage dans les intrants du méthaniseur : cet indicateur a également été élaboré à partir des données quantitatives des exploitants et permet de quantifier l’importance des effluents et donc de l’élevage dans la ration du méthaniseur. 4. Indicateur d’évolution des cultures principales : Cet indicateur, fondé sur les analyses précédentes, a été formalisé en utilisant les résultats statistiques des boxplots des cultures suivantes (blé, maïs grain, orge, maïs ensilage, colza, prairie. Les valeurs au sein de la boîte (Q$_1$ à Q$_3$) ont été codées comme 0 (aucun ou faible changement), celles dans les moustaches comme 1 (changement modéré), et les outliers comme 2 (changement significatif). Au final, cet indicateur est égal à la somme des valeurs (0, 1, ou 2) pour chaque culture considérée. Cette catégorisation permet de quantifier les ajustements des systèmes de culture. 5. Indicateur d’évolution des productions animales : L’évolution des productions animales a été évaluée en fonction des effectifs. Un code 0 est attribué lorsque l’effectif reste stable, 1 en cas d’augmentation du cheptel, et 2 en cas d’arrêt ou diminution significative de l’atelier de production. [SOURCE: Page_95]

Une Analyse en Composantes Principales (ACP) (figure 37) a ensuite été réalisée pour identifier les regroupements d’exploitations en fonction de ces indicateurs. Cette méthode a permis de révéler des dynamiques spécifiques au sein des exploitations étudiées, mettant en lumière les changements structurels induits par la méthanisation. [SOURCE: Page_95]

95 [SOURCE: Page_95]

Chapitre 2 [SOURCE: Page_96]

Figure 37. Analyse en composante principale des exploitations enquêtées et classification selon les logiques d’évolution. [SOURCE: Page_96]

(Les exploitations en jaune représentent les exploitations porcines, et les exploitations en marron représentent les exploitations bovines). [SOURCE: Page_96]

Logique 1 : Stabilité des pratiques agricoles avec ajout d’une dimension énergétique à tendance territoriale Dans certaines exploitations bovines et porcines (GE_1, ND_1, B_2, Aq_1), la méthanisation n’entraine pas nécessairement une transformation du système d’élevage ou de culture. Au lieu de cela, la méthanisation s’ajoute comme une nouvelle pratique qui crée de la synergie avec les cultures et l’élevage existant. L’agriculteur vise à améliorer la gestion de ses effluents d’élevage qu’il utilise comme intrant du méthansieur et il fertilise les cultures avec le digestat produit. Ces effluents représentent une part importante des intrants de l’exploitation et la pression de l’approvisionnement du méthaniseur est gérée par l’importation des déchets extérieurs, ce qui permet de minimiser la mise en place de CIVE et la modification associée des cultures. Les exploitations ont plutôt adopté la méthanisation récemment, la date moyenne de la mise en œuvre de l’unité de méthanisation est 2016 [2011, 2021]. [SOURCE: Page_96]

Logique 2: Réorganisation des pratiques culturales pour maximiser la production énergétique issue de l’exploitation [SOURCE: Page_96]

96 [SOURCE: Page_96]

Chapitre 2 [SOURCE: Page_97]

Cette logique caractérise les exploitations qui réorganisent leurs pratiques culturales pour atteindre une bonne production énergétique. Il s’agit des exploitations bovines et porcines (Bzh_1, Bzh_3, GE_5, CVL_1, B_1), qui introduisent des CIVE en proportion significative pour alimenter le méthaniseur et qui modifient un peu les cultures principales pour faciliter cette insertion. Ce qui veut dire que ces exploitations changent les rotations et optimisent le digestat pour la fertilisation des cultures et des CIVE. Ainsi, les exploitants de cette logique ont tendance à faire disparaitre les cultures qui ne s’adaptent pas à l’insertion des CIVE et les cultures industrielles qu’ils ne jugent pas rentables par rapport à la méthanisation et prennent du temps pour leur gestion. Ils les arrêtent et se concentrent ainsi sur la méthanisation. La part d’intrants extérieurs dans le méthaniseur de ces exploitations est relativement faible et la part d’effluents d’élevage est également modérée. Dans cette logique, la priorité est la rentabilité liée à la production de biogaz. Par exemple, pour une exploitation porcine, l’exploitant récolte le maïs grain en maïs ensilage pour le méthaniseur et importe ensuite de l’alimentation pour les animaux car il juge que c’est plus rentable. Les exploitations sont plutôt anciennes, la date moyenne de mise en œuvre de la méthanisation est 2008 [2004, 2010]. Les exploitations (Bzh_5, Bzh_6 et Bzh_7) n’ayant pas été enquêtées de façon quantitative peuvent toutefois être qualitativement classées dans cette logique. Les agriculteurs, remplacent les couverts déjà présents (CIPAN) dans leur exploitation et importent de l’alimentation animale car le maïs grain est également partiellement récolté en maïs ensilage pour nourrir le méthaniseur. Ces exploitations sont bovines et porcines avec methaniseur récent (2021). [SOURCE: Page_97]

Logique 3 : Réorganisation du système d’élevage pour la production d’énergie Les exploitations bovines et porcines de cette logique sont celles pour lesquelles la méthanisation entraine une réorganisation du système d’élevage (GE_3, GE_2, Bzh_2, Bzh_4, PDL_1). Cette logique se traduit par une réduction du cheptel pour avoir plus de temps ou/et de surface pour l’unité de méthanisation. Ces exploitations peuvent compléter la ration du méthaniseur par l’ajout d’intrants extérieurs ou une part modérée de CIVE mais modifient peu le système de culture. Pour cette logique, on retrouve des déterminants identiques à la logique 2 (rentabilité et temps de travail) mais avec un choix d’adaptation différent, qui conduit plutôt à diminuer ou arrêter une activité d’élevage pour la remplacer par la méthanisation aussi bien en termes de temps de travail, de rentabilité économique et d’exutoire pour les cultures. Par exemple, une exploitation a perdu des associés, donc elle a décidé de diminuer l’élevage, elle se retrouve avec beaucoup d’herbe à gérer et la méthanisation était la solution. Ils trouvent que l’élevage n’est pas aussi rentable que la méthanisation. Et la réduction est réalisé en sorte que la quantité d’effluents ne soit pas trop réduite et que le digesteur soit constamment alimenté. Les exploitations sont plutôt avec des méthaniseurs anciens, la date de mise en œuvre de l’unité de méthanisation est 2012 [2008, 2017]. La répartition des exploitations dans les différentes logiques est présentée dans le tableau 10 en termes de proportion d’exploitations et d’intrants méthanisés. Nous pouvons constater que la majorité des [SOURCE: Page_97]

97 [SOURCE: Page_97]

Chapitre 2 [SOURCE: Page_98]

exploitations se concentre dans la logique 2 et 3. Tandis que la proportion d’intrants méthanisés est plus remarquable dans la logique 1 et 3. [SOURCE: Page_98]

### 2.3.6. Autres enseignements des enquêtes

Les enquêtes ont permis de soulever les contraintes et perspectives des agriculteurs. La diversification des activités et les raisons économiques ont été les principales motivations pour l'introduction de la méthanisation citées par les exploitants. Au-delà de ces motivations, l'amélioration de la gestion des effluents d'élevage est mentionnée comme un point important, notamment vis-à-vis des nuisances olfactives. Plusieurs agriculteurs expriment des craintes face à la multiplication des unités de méthanisation dans leur région, provoquant une concurrence accrue pour des intrants auparavant gratuits, mais désormais devenus payants. Ils s'interrogent également sur l'avenir de la méthanisation après la fin des contrats de cogénération. L'intérêt croissant des industriels pour la méthanisation suscite également des inquiétudes quant à la pérennité de leurs pratiques et leur compétitivité sur le marché. Par ailleurs, les agriculteurs ont constaté qu'ils utilisent moins de chaux et ont mentionné qu'ils observent une augmentation de la matière organique dans les sols, due à la fertilisation avec les digestats. Ils mentionnent également une réduction des herbicides et fongicides, notamment grâce à la présence des CIVE. [SOURCE: Page_98]

### 2.4. Conclusion

Les résultats des enquêtes nous ont permis de mieux caractériser les changements induits dans les systèmes agricoles par l’insertion d’une unité de méthanisation. Faute de temps, nous n’avons pas pu enquêter davantage d'exploitations, ce qui signifie que certaines pratiques ont peut-être échappé à notre analyse, étant donné que nous n’avons pas couvert toute la diversité des unités de méthanisation existantes dans les régions étudiées et que notre échantillon reste modeste. [SOURCE: Page_98]

Nous avons complété ces lacunes par des recherches bibliographiques (chapitre 1) et, grâce aux échanges lors de séminaires et conférences, nous avons réussi à obtenir une vision plus globale des [SOURCE: Page_98]

Chapitre 2 [SOURCE: Page_99]

changements de pratiques. Cependant, seule une partie de ces changements a été simulée, et le plus souvent amplifiée (chapitre 3 et 4) pour simuler l'impact environnemental de ces changements. [SOURCE: Page_99]

Ces logiques d’action montrent que la méthanisation, en fonction du contexte et des priorités des exploitations, peut avoir un impact très variable sur les pratiques agricoles. Elle peut être intégrée sans changement majeur (Logique 1), devenir un moteur de réorganisation des cultures et de production énergétique (Logique 2), ou encore influencer directement la gestion du cheptel (Logique 3). Ces différentes approches permettent aux exploitants d'adapter leur système de production aux enjeux de la transition énergétique. [SOURCE: Page_99]

A partir de ces logiques plusieurs enseignements ont été tirés. Dans notre échantillon, les exploitations bovines utilisent modérément les CIVE pour l’alimentation du méthaniseur en modifiant peu les assolements. Ils ont plutôt tendance à rediriger partiellement des cultures existantes (fourrages et/ou prairies) vers le méthaniseur en diminuant parfois le cheptel pour gérer la compétition. Au contraire, les exploitations porcines insèrent plus massivement les CIVE avec des changements plus importants mais toutefois modérés dans les systèmes de culture. Au-delà du type d’exploitation, la part d’intrants extérieurs à l’exploitation semble également un facteur déterminant dans l’évolution des exploitations avec une part de CIVE d’autant plus importante que les intrants extérieurs sont faibles. Chapitre 3 [SOURCE: Page_102]

Chapitre 3. Impacts sur la qualité de l’eau de la production de biogaz dans les élevages porcins en fonction des pratiques agricoles associées. [SOURCE: Page_102]

Ce chapitre 3 est écrit sous forme d’une publication scientifique publiée dans la revue Environnemental Research Communication (ERC). [SOURCE: Page_102]

Ouarda Baziz^1. Fabrice Beline 1. Patrick Durand 1. 1 INRAE, Institut Agro Rennes-Anger, SAS, 35000 Rennes, France [SOURCE: Page_102]

Résumé [SOURCE: Page_102]

L'objectif de cette étude était d'évaluer les impacts positifs ou négatifs de la digestion anaérobie (DA) sur la qualité de l'eau en utilisant une approche systémique. À cette fin, nous avons utilisé le modèle agro-hydrologique Topography-based Nitrogen Transfer and Transformation (TNT$_2$), un modèle explicite spatialement qui simule les flux d'azote et d'eau à l'échelle du bassin versant sur une base quotidienne. Quatre scénarios ont été construits et analysés : un scénario de référence avant l'introduction de la DA (S$_0$), un scénario avec DA et avec une fertilisation ajustée (S$_1$), un scénario avec DA et avec une fertilisation non ajustée (S$_2$), et un scénario DA agroécologique (S$_3$). Les résultats ont montré que, lorsque les pratiques d'épandage étaient similaires et qu'une quantité équivalente d'azote efficace était appliquée, le lisier de porc digéré avait généralement une quantité prédite de lixiviation de nitrate similaire à celle du lisier de porc non digéré. De plus, remplacer les cultures intermédiaires pièges à nitrate par des cultures intermédiaires à vocation énergétique avait peu d'impact sur la qualité de l'eau. Le scénario S$_3$ était le plus favorable pour la qualité de l'eau et la production de biogaz, mais pas pour le stockage de l'azote organique du sol et la production alimentaire et fourragère. La force de cette étude réside dans son approche systémique, qui a pris en compte à la fois les aspects environnementaux et agronomiques pour évaluer les scénarios. [SOURCE: Page_102]

Abstract [SOURCE: Page_102]

The aim of this study was to assess positive or negative impacts of anaerobic digestion (AD) on water quality using a systemic approach. To this end, we used the agro-hydrological model Topography-based Nitrogen Transfer and Transformation (TNT$_2$), a spatially explicit model that simulates nitrogen and water flows at the watershed scale on a daily time step. Four scenarios were constructed and analyzed: a baseline before the introduction of AD (S$_0$), AD with adjusted fertilization (S$_1$), AD with unadjusted fertilization (S$_2$), and agroecological AD (S$_3$). The results showed that, when spreading practices were similar and an equivalent amount of effective nitrogen was applied, digested pig slurry generally had a predicted amount of nitrate leaching similar to that of undigested pig slurry. In addition, replacing catch crops with energy cover crops had little impact on water quality. Scenario S$_3$ was the most favorable [SOURCE: Page_102]

102 [SOURCE: Page_102]

Chapitre 3 [SOURCE: Page_103]

one for water quality and biogas production, but not for soil organic nitrogen storage and food and feed production. This study’s strength is its systemic approach, which considered both environmental and agronomic aspects to assess the scenarios. Keywords: anaerobic digestion, water quality, TNT$_2$, systemic approach, watershed [SOURCE: Page_103]

### 3.1. Introduction

The history of anaerobic digestion (AD) dates back several centuries, with the first records describing combustible gas produced by decomposing organic waste (Abbasi et al., 2012). AD is a biological process in which organic matter decomposes in an anaerobic environment and produces biogas composed mainly of methane (CH$_4$) and carbon dioxide (Ahring, 2003). This decomposition can produce renewable energy from waste and help contribute to carbon neutrality (Ward et al., 2008). AD is developing in livestock farms, which produce a large amount of organic waste as manure, slurry, and crop residues. Livestock waste is one of the main concerns regarding the degradatio n of water quality (Hooda et al., 2000) since it contains large amounts of nitrogen (N) (Follett and Hatfield, 2001) and phosphorus (Knowlton et al., 2004), which can leach into rivers and groundwater. In addition to producing renewable energy, AD also generates a residue as a by-product called 'digestate', which is composed of minerals and non-decomposed organic matter. Digestate can replace organic and inorganic fertilizers and other soil additives (Westerman et al., 2012). The AD industry is growing rapidly. Germany is the leading biogas producer in Europe and the world, with more than 9000 digesters (Sesini et al., 2023), while France ranks third, with more 1400 digesters. French public policies support AD development in order to meet the national objectives of renewable energy production and carbon neutrality. Indeed, AD plays a crucial role in French energy prospects and ECC and fodder (including grassland) are the main substrates used to fulfil such objective (ADEME, 2021). Thereby from 50 to 60 and 14 to 20 Twh/year could be produced by energy catch crops (ECC) and fodder, respectively, through AD in 2050 for a total energy production of AD between 113 and 151 TWh/year from the agriculture sector (Beline et al., 2023). Initially developed using livestock and food processing waste, AD development is actually largely based on ECC and in a lesser extent on fodder such as maize and grass (Launay et al., 2022). The introduction of ECC in AD systems is widely used by several countries (Szerencsits et al., 2016, Strauß et al., 2019, Riau et al 2021, Levavasseur et al., 2023b), mainly to ensure a continuous supply of substrate for biogas units without the need to rely on main crops and without competing with food production (Styles et al., 2015). Unlike catch crops (CC), they are usually fertilized and sometimes irrigated. Despite their potential for maximizing agronomic and environmental benefits (Launay et al., 2022), they are sometimes suspected of competing with the main crops and being less effective than CC in preventing NO 3 leaching. AD can also be considered as a technology that adds value to fodder crops (e.g. grassland, legumes (alfalfa) and thus may increase their cultivation, even on farms without ruminants, as predicted by forecasting studies (Couturier et al., [SOURCE: Page_103]

103 [SOURCE: Page_103]

Chapitre 3 [SOURCE: Page_104]

2016). In Germany, 30 to 40% of biogas plants use grass from permanent grasslands as feedstock for the digester (Elsäßer et al., 2012). Such changes could improve water quality. For sustainable management of anaerobic digestion, developing other biomasses that do not compete with food crops is crucial (Bedoić et al., 2019). However, the development of AD raises questions about environmental externalities and the associated risks of air, soil, and water pollution. While AD is often seen as a solution to improve the management of agricultural waste, particularly livestock waste, by replacing it with a more manageable digestate, the resulting impact of the series of changes to farm management entailed by an AD project remain under debate (Möller, 2015). Several studies have examined impacts of digestate on water quality at the field scale (Pötsch, 2004, Möller and Stinner, 2009; Svoboda et al., 2013; Nicholson et al., 2017). Although their results varied, few differences in the risks of nitrate (NO 3) leaching were observed between different types of livestock waste (whether digested or not) and inorganic fertilizers. However, digestate compositions and characteristics vary widely. Thus, to minimize the risks of NO 3 leaching, it is important to consider the composition of each type of digestate and adapt spreading practices accordingly. Since the characteristics of the inputs used in AD influence the quality of the digestate (Jimenez et al., 2019), and these inputs depend on farmers' management practices, a comprehensive systemic approach is necessary to assess the environmental risks of AD properly. There are more reasons advocating for a systemic approach. First, the flux of nitrate at the outlet of a catchment, even a small one, is generally not the sum of the leaching of each field: many processes related to spatial interactions, buffering, transit time, mixing of different aquifers, etc, result in a nitrate retention depending on the agricultural and physiographic context (e.g. Ruiz et al., 2002, Abbott et al 2018, Ehrhardt et al., 2019, Dupas et al., 2020). Therefore, if an AD project modifies the distribution of crop rotations in the landscape, the resulting effect will be different that what it observed at field scale. Second, ECC can only be introduced in specific intercropping sequences, when the period of time between the harvest of the preceding crop and the sowing of the new one is long enough (e.g. between wheat and maize), whereas CC implantation is more flexible. Therefore, the proportion of CC and ECC and their resulting effect on nitrate concentrations can only be assessed at the farm or landscape scale. Third, AD implies more circularity in a farming system and different management of the crops, by- products and wastes, which may have consequences on the productivity, feed/food ratio, carbon sequestration, etc of the farm, and it is important to balance the positive or negative effects of AD on water pollution by gain or losses on other functions of the farm system. Among all the farm systems, indoor pig breeding farms have often been identified as a major source of nitrogen pollution, since they concentrate large fluxes of nitrogen on a proportionally limited area (Sutton et al., 2011). Indeed, hot spots of nitrogen pollution in the world, either atmospheric or aquatic, are associated to high density of livestock in general and often pig farming (Erisman et al., 2014, Billen et al., 2011, de Vries et al., 2011). Pig farming is often associated with cereal cropping such as maize, [SOURCE: Page_104]

104 [SOURCE: Page_104]

Chapitre 3 [SOURCE: Page_105]

wheat and barley fertilized with the manure and used to feeding the animals. This is typically the case in Europe, one of the major pig producing region in the world. Within Europe, Brittany, Western France, which produces 5% of the European pig production on less than 1% of the area, is representative of these hot spots, where AD develops rapidly to improve animal waste management. This study aimed to analyze the combined effects of changes induced by AD on a farm's operating system or a group of farms. Unlike other studies, this study adopted a systemic approach at the farm scale rather than the field scale and attempted to consider all changes caused by introducing AD that could influence water quality. The underlying hypothesis was that these systemic changes have larger impacts, whether positive or negative, than does changing the types of fertilizers alone. Given the many management practices available to farmers who invest in AD, the difficulty and cost of setting up a real-world experiment, and the time lag between a change in management practices and observable effects on stream water quality (Guillaumot et al., 2021; Malik et al., 2022), we adopted a scenario-based modeling approach. The objectives of this study were to (1) construct contrasting scenarios based on changes generated at the farm level by introduction AD to a pig farm, (2) predict impacts of introducing ECC to the cropping system on N flows and especially water quality, and (3) predict impacts of introducing permanent grassland into the cropping system. The scenarios constructed were simulated using the agro-hydrological model Topography-based Nitrogen Transfer and Transformations (TNT$_2$) (Beaujouan et al., 2002) along with simple, data-based farm and AD modeling approaches. [SOURCE: Page_105]

### 3.2.1. Study site and challenges of the approach

The Kervidy-Naizin watershed in Brittany (figure 38), western France, is associated with AgrHyS, a widely recognized long-term observatory in the fields of agriculture and the environment (Fovet et al 2018). It covers 4.9 km^2 and is drained by the Coët-Dan River, a tributary of the Evel River. It is characterized by intensive agriculture that combines mixed cropping (i.e., cereals, maize, grassland, and vegetables) and livestock farming (i.e., cattle, pigs, and poultry) on relatively moderate slopes (5%). Approximately 91% of the area is used for agricultural purposes. Considered as an outdoor laboratory, the watershed is equipped with water monitoring devices, soil sensors, and other equipment to measure and record environmental parameters. It was chosen in the present study due to its long-term time series of quantitative and qualitative data: streamflow, daily NO 3 concentrations, cropping practices, and characteristics of the climate, soil, topography, and agricultural system. The watershed has a temperate oceanic climate, with mean annual precipitation of 827 mm and a daily mean temperature of 11.2 °C. Its mean annual specific runoff is 314 mm, with seasonal variability. The dominant soils are clay loams 60–80 cm deep whose drainage depends on the slope. The watershed's [SOURCE: Page_105]

105 [SOURCE: Page_105]

Chapitre 3 [SOURCE: Page_106]

elevation ranges from 98 m at the outlet to 140 m. AgrHys provides a large amount of data online (Ozcar- RI, 2021), see Gascuel-Odoux et al. (2018) for a history of the Kervidy-Naizin watershed. One of the main characteristics of these data is the high NO$_3$ concentration in stream water: ca. 16.5 mg L−1 N−1- NO$_3$. This study simulated N dynamics at the watershed scale by representing the processes involved in the 'soil-plant' compartment and in the hydrological pathways. The principle was to construct a 'virtual' farm whose fields would cover an entire sub-watershed of the Kervidy-Naizin watershed, and to compare different scenarios with and without AD implementation. The Kéramiot sub-watershed, drained by an upstream reach of the Kervidy, was chosen to represent the farm. Existing data on the farming operations of this sub-watershed were replaced with data from a virtual pig farm based on real soil, crop, and practice characteristics. The farm covered the entire sub-watershed area (i.e., 104 ha) and produced 5363 finishing pigs per year. The number of pigs on the farm was set to meet the regulatory limit of 170 kg spreadable organic N/ha/yr, based on the emission factor of 3.17 kg spreadable N/pig.yr (Légifrance, 2016). We used weather data recorded from 1995–2020 at the study site to drive the simulation. Simulating this system required representing agricultural practices (e.g., crop rotations, fertilization) before and after the introduction of AD. To this end, a survey was conducted of four farms in the municipality of Naizin that recently developed a collective AD project. [SOURCE: Page_106]

### 3.2.2. Presentation of the models

3.2.2.1. Soil and groundwater nitrogen fluxes modelling by the tool Topography Nitrogen Tranfert and Transformations (TNT$_2$) [SOURCE: Page_107]

TNT$_2$ was used to simulate the fate of N in soil-crop-groundwater system scenarios (figure 39). It is a spatially explicit agro-hydrological model (Beaujouan et al., 2001) that combines a hydrological model inspired by the model TOPMODEL (Beven, 1997) with the agronomic crop and soil model STICS (Simulateur mulTIdisciplinaire pour les cultures standard) (Brisson et al 1998). As such, it is designed to model plant growth and N transfer and transformations in an agricultural watershed and plant growth based on soil and climate conditions on a daily time step (Beaujouan et al., 2002). Thus, it also predicts NO$_3$ leaching, which can percolate below the soil and move in groundwater to the river. STICS, developed at INRAE in France since 1996, simulates crop growth and soil water and N balances. It follows a mechanistic approach that describes the biophysical processes that govern plant growth and exchanges between crops and their environment (Brisson et al., 2003). STICS simulates crop growth using daily weather data to drive water and N balances of the soil, as well as interactions between roots and soil. It can predict agricultural variables (e.g., yield, input consumption) and environmental variables (e.g., water dynamics, N losses) to help assess agronomic and environmental performances of crops. It can be adapted to different crops using relevant generic parameters for most crops and options in the model equations to represent the physiology and management of each crop. The hydrological submodel of TNT$_2$ is based on the assumption of TOPMODEL. It represents darcian saturated flow, a hydraulic gradient that is constant and equal to the topographic slope, hydraulic transmissivity that decreases exponentially as soil depth increases, runoff generated on saturated areas at valley bottoms, and dynamics described by a succession of steady states (Beven and Kirkby, 1979). TNT$_2$ uses a fixed grid to represent the watershed, with each cell divided into horizontal slices of varying thickness to distinguish immobile water from mobile water (Beaujouan et al., 2002). The flows are vertical (downwards or upwards) in the unsaturated zone and lateral in the saturated zone, with multidirectional flows between neighboring cells at lower elevations. The topography of the watershed (calculated from a digital elevation model) is particularly important in the model, since it determines the distribution of surface and groundwater flows (Beven et al., 2021). TNT$_2$ simulates Hortonian runoff to represent the presence of poorly permeable areas. Hydrological parameters include maximum transmissivity, the exponential decay coefficient of transmissivity, retention and drainage porosities, the thicknesses of each layer considered, and infiltration rate, differentiated by soil type. To connect the two models, the STICS model was slightly simplified and integrated into the TNT$_2$ code using C++ (GNU Genral Public License (GNU GPL)). We used the parameters that had been calibrated in previous studies of the Kervidy-Naizin watershed (Casal et al., 2019). Since the present study compared multiple scenarios, no calibration or validation was performed, and we assumed that the many studies that had used TNT$_2$ in similar contexts [SOURCE: Page_107]

107 [SOURCE: Page_107]

Chapitre 3 [SOURCE: Page_108]

provided sufficient confidence in the applicability and robustness of the model. The scenarios were simulated for 25 years (1995–2020) using the climate data record for Agrhys observatory. [SOURCE: Page_108]

Figure 39. Diagramm of the TNT$_2$ model. ETP: evapotranspiration. [SOURCE: Page_108]

### 3.2.3. Modelling of nitrogen fluxes from animals and the effluents management

Because pigs had a strong influence on the farm's N dynamics, N input and output flows related to pig production were modelled using reference data used by the French administration (Légifrance, 2016) to estimate N flows at the farm level. For the pig system, N enters through the purchase of post-weaning piglets and leaves through pig excretions, gaseous emissions, and the sale of fattened pigs. The piglets have a mean liveweight of 31 kg upon arrival (Légifrance, 2016) and are sold at 118 kg. To calculate the corresponding N input and output flows from animals, the following equation was used from Dourmad et al. (2016): number of pigs/yr × 0.02456 (kg N/kg) × liveweight (kg). N excretion was estimated assuming that each pig excreted 4.56 kg N/pig.yr (CORPEN, 2004), while gaseous N emissions were calculated as excreted N minus spreadable N. Spreadable N amount comes from (Légifrance, 2016), and is equal to 3.17 kg N/pig.yr. These N losses correspond mainly to ammonia (NH$_3$) emissions from building and storage. We assumed that gaseous N emissions did not differ among scenarios (i.e., regardless of the presence of AD), assuming that AD did not influence gaseous emissions from buildings and influenced those from storage only slightly. Indeed, raw livestock waste is stored for a shorter period with AD, which decreases associated gaseous emissions, than without AD. However, emission after AD due to the increase in the ammonium content and pH are higher, which results in an unclear trend in gaseous N emissions for the entire chain. Both livestock waste and digestate could be covered during storage, which would reduce their gaseous emissions. The amount of N in the feed was calculated as the N retained plus N excreted by the pigs. [SOURCE: Page_108]

108 [SOURCE: Page_108]

### 3.2.4. Modelling of anaerobic digestion

Predicting biogas production was of paramount importance since it assessed the energy-production potential of each AD scenario. The biogas production in each scenario was calculated using data from the French MéthaSim database (IFIP, 2021), which provided the characteristics of each input (table 11). The biogaz production was assumed to be 80% of biochemical methane potential. [SOURCE: Page_109]

Table 11. Characteristics of the inputs for the anaerobic digester in the simulated scenarios. All data came from the MéthaSim database. BMP: biochemical methane potential, ECC: energy cover crop, NH$_4$: ammonium content. [SOURCE: Page_109]

Naming inputs             BMP                 Dry         Organic       Total N          NH$_4$ (m^3 CH$_4$/t            matter       matter        (kg N/t)       (kgN/t) Organic matter)        (kg/t)        (kg/t) [SOURCE: Page_109]

Pig slurry                   323              44.3            33.2          3.7         2.22 Maize stalks                 294              793            750.9          8.7          0.2 Cereal straw                 261              873             73.4          4.7         1.23 Green rye (ECC)              389              197            181.0          4.3         1.38 Grass                        303              158             140           4.8         0.01 [SOURCE: Page_109]

The amount of digestate (t fresh matter) was calculated by summing all of the inputs and then allocated to the crops on the farm. N enters the digester in organic form and exits mainly in the form of reduced inorganic N (NH$_3$). The total amount of N that enters AD equals the amount that exits, but the content of inorganic N increases after AD, while that of organic N decreases (Quideau et al., 2013). The mineral content in the digestate was estimated at 75% for scenarios [SOURCE: Page_109]

### 3.2.5. Description of the scenarios

Four scenarios were constructed to explore different options possible for a pig farm when adopting AD. We considered here changes that have already been observed in real farms and strived to make the scenarios as comparable as possible with respect to dimensions not directly related to AD. In particular, the total area of the farm and the number of pigs produced were kept constant. The four scenarios considered were: a baseline before the introduction of AD (S$_0$), AD with adjusted fertilization (S$_1$), AD with unadjusted fertilization (S$_2$), and agroecological AD (S$_3$) (table 12). [SOURCE: Page_109]

109 [SOURCE: Page_109]

3.2.5.1 Baseline scenario without anaerobic digestion (S$_0$) [SOURCE: Page_110]

The baseline scenario (S$_0$) was based on current data from pig farms without AD (figure 40). The cropping system consists of crop rotations used to produce pig feed (35% grain maize, 30% wheat, and 10% barley, overall) or sold as cash crops (10% potatoes, 10% green beans, and 5% rapeseed, overall). In compliance with current regulations in Brittany, CC are grown between the main crops (ryegrass between winter and spring crops, mustard between winter crops), not fertilized, and buried at the end of their cycle. The residues of the main crops (including stalks) are also buried, and the straw is sold. The main crops are fertilized with pig slurry (at 66% for mineral content) and inorganic fertilizers up to the regulatory limits of total N load to the soil of ca. 210 kg ha−1 yr−1 and organic N load of 170 kg/ha/yr. All excess slurry is exported. For this scenario, an NH$_3$ emission factor at spreading of 5% or 17% was used when the slurry was buried or not buried, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_110]

Figure 40. Nitrogen flows considered in scenario S$_0$ [SOURCE: Page_110]

### 3.2.5.2. Scenario with anaerobic digestion and adjusted fertilization (S$_1$)

In scenario S$_1$, a digester was introduced into the farm (figure 41). The cropping system is similar to S$_0$ as much as possible, but when possible, the CC are replaced with the ECC (i.e., green rye, commonly grown as an ECC in Brittany). Replacing CC with an ECC aims to avoid competition with main crops, but the growth cycle of certain ones, such as maize, still needed to be shortened to insure a sufficient growing time for the ECC. The remaining CC (during short periods between crops) were still buried. The residues of the main crops (including stalks) are also buried and the straw is sold. The crop residues, ECC, and pig manure are fed into the digester. The main crops are fertilized with raw liquid digestate and inorganic fertilizers. The ECC was fertilized with raw liquid digestate at ca. 100 kg N/ha. To meet the regulatory limits and have the same total N load to the soil as in S$_0$, fertilization of the main crops [SOURCE: Page_110]

110 [SOURCE: Page_110]

Chapitre 3 [SOURCE: Page_111]

was reduced accordingly. For this and the other AD scenarios (S$_2$ and S$_3$), an NH$_3$ emission factor of 13% or 25% was used when the digestate was buried or not buried, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_111]

### 3.2.5.2. Scenario with anaerobic digestion and unadjusted fertilization (S$_2$)

In scenario S$_2$, the system was similar to that in S$_1$, but fertilization of the main crops was not reduced, which increased the total N load to the soil (figure 41). The objective was to simulate a relatively common situation in which the farmer does not consider the increased N load caused by fertilizing ECC. All excess digestate was exported. [SOURCE: Page_111]

Figure 41. Nitrogen flows considered in scenario S$_1$ and S$_2$ [SOURCE: Page_111]

### 3.2.5.3. Agroecological scenario S$_3$

Agroecology is an approach to agriculture that aims to develop ecologically sustainable and socially equitable agricultural production systems (Wezel et al., 2009). It integrates agricultural practices that minimize the use of chemicals and promote biodiversity while improving soil quality and long-term productivity (Altieri 2018). Among these practices, permanent grassland can be introduced into a farm with AD and its biomass fed into the digester. Thus, the grassland is used as an ECC (McEniry and O'Kiely, 2013), as in other bio-energetic systems (Prochnow et al., 2009). In scenario S$_3$ (figure 42), ca. 10% of the watershed area near the stream was converted into permanent grassland composed of a mixture of ryegrass and clover. This grassland was not fertilized and was mown four times per year (i.e., May, June, July, and October). [SOURCE: Page_111]

111 [SOURCE: Page_111]

Chapitre 3 [SOURCE: Page_112]

Figure 42. Nitrogen flows considered in scenario S$_3$ [SOURCE: Page_112]

The location (figure 43) near the stream was chosen because of its potential to intercept N leached from upslope (Casal et al., 2019). [SOURCE: Page_112]

Figure 43. Location of grassland plot in Kéramiot-sub-watershed [SOURCE: Page_112]

Index                                 Description and equation Gross NUE        N-use efficiency of the farm: = (𝑁𝑐𝑟𝑜𝑝𝑠 + 𝑁𝑝𝑖𝑔𝑠 + 𝑁𝑤𝑎𝑠𝑡𝑒 )/(𝑁𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑡 + 𝑁𝑎𝑡𝑚 + 𝑁𝑝𝑝 + 𝑁𝑝𝑓𝑒𝑒𝑑) Soil NUE        N-use efficiency of the cropping system: = (𝑁𝑐𝑟𝑜𝑝𝑠 + 𝑁𝑎𝑓𝑒𝑒𝑑 + 𝑁𝑒𝑐)/(𝑁𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑡 + 𝑁𝑜𝑟𝑔 + 𝑁𝑎𝑡𝑚 + 𝑁𝑏𝑓) Nw/Nin         Proportion of the total N input lost to water: = 𝑁𝑛𝑜3/(𝑁𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑡 + 𝑁𝑎𝑡𝑚 + 𝑁𝑏𝑓 + 𝑁𝑝𝑝 + 𝑁𝑝𝑓𝑒𝑒𝑑) Natm/Nin        Proportion of the total N input lost to the atmosphere: = (𝑁𝑛ℎ3 + 𝑁𝑑𝑒𝑛𝑖𝑡)/(𝑁𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑡 + 𝑁𝑎𝑡𝑚 + 𝑁𝑏𝑓 + 𝑁𝑝𝑝 + 𝑁𝑝𝑓𝑒𝑒𝑑) Nexpfood/Nin Proportion of the total N input transformed into food products: = (𝑁𝑐𝑟𝑜𝑝𝑠 + 𝑁𝑝𝑖𝑔𝑠)/(𝑁𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑡 + 𝑁𝑎𝑡𝑚 + 𝑁𝑝𝑝 + 𝑁𝑝𝑓𝑒𝑒𝑑) Biog/Nin       Biogas production per kg of N input: = 𝐵𝑖𝑜𝑔/(𝑁𝑓𝑒𝑟𝑡 + 𝑁𝑎𝑡𝑚 + 𝑁𝑏𝑓 + 𝑁𝑝𝑝 + 𝑁𝑝𝑓𝑒𝑒𝑑) N org soil      Organic soil nitrogen conservation : = 0 if ∆N > 0: = -∆N/(Ncrop + Nafeed + Nec) if ∆N < 0 [SOURCE: Page_114]

### 3.3.1. Impact of scenarios on water quality at the outlet

After 25 simulated years, cumulative NO 3 losses at the outlet of scenarios S$_1$ and S$_2$ were 1.89% and 12.2% larger, respectively, than those of S$_0$, while those of S$_3$ were 15.1% lower (figure 44). Differences among the scenarios became visible after five years, and seemed to stabilize after 20 years. The average mean annual flow for the last five years for the four scenarios are, respectively, 30 Kg N/ha, 31 Kg N/ha, 35 Kg N/ha and 25 Kg N/ha [SOURCE: Page_114]

114 [SOURCE: Page_114]

Chapitre 3 [SOURCE: Page_115]

Figure 44. Cumulative nitrogen flow at the outlet of the sub-watershed predicted by TNT$_2$ from 1995- 2020 [SOURCE: Page_115]

Since the water flux at the outlet is similar for the four scenarios, these differences are also visible for the concentrations (figure 45). The average concentration for the last five years for the four scenarios (S$_0$, S$_1$, S$_2$ and S$_3$) are, respectively 46 g/m^2 , 46 g/m^2, 53 g/m^2 and 36 g/m^2 [SOURCE: Page_115]

115 [SOURCE: Page_115]

Chapitre 3 [SOURCE: Page_116]

Figure 45. Mean nitrogen concentration at the outlet of the sub-watershed predicted by TNT$_2$ from 1995-2020 [SOURCE: Page_116]

### 3.3.2. Impact of scenarios on total nitrogen flow at the farm level

Annual N inflows to and outflows from the soil for the scenarios were represented using Sankey diagrams (figure 46). The N inflows included atmospheric deposition, biological N fixation, inorganic fertilization, and organic fertilization (pig slurry in S$_0$ and digestate in S$_1$, S$_2$, and S$_3$). Inorganic fertilization was relatively similar among the scenarios. By construction, organic fertilization was highest in S$_2$ and lowest in S$_3$, while biological N fixation was the highest in S$_3$ due to the grassland legumes. Denitrification in S$_0$ was slightly higher than that in S$_1$ and S$_3$ and nearly equivalent to that in S$_2$ (ca. 18, 16, 15, and 19 kg N/ha/yr, respectively). NH$_3$ volatilization from the fields was highest in S$_2$, followed by S$_0$, S$_1$, and S$_3$ (ca. 21, 18, 17, and 15 kg N/ha/yr, respectively). NH$_3$ losses in the building and during storage were the same for all four scenarios (74 kg N/ha/yr) because they had the same number of pigs, and we assumed that emissions from slurry and digestate did not differ during storage. Organic N and carbon were stored in the soil in S$_0$, while soil organic matter decreased in the other three scenarios, but less so in S$_3$ than in S$_1$ and S$_2$. The assumption that stalks are left in the field in S$_0$ but fed to the digester in the other scenarios explains the increase in N export in harvested cereals in the latter. Specifically, grain production decreased slightly in S$_2$ but more in S$_3$ and S$_1$. In addition, S$_3$ produced 5% more biogas than did S$_1$ and S$_2$, which produced similar amounts. [SOURCE: Page_116]

116 [SOURCE: Page_116]

Chapitre 3 [SOURCE: Page_117]

Figure 46. Sankey diagrams of annual nitrogen dynamics for scenarios (a) S$_0$, (b) S$_1$, (c) S$_2$, and (d) S$_3$ [SOURCE: Page_117]

The dotted frame represent the boundaries of pig farming, and the arrows represent input, output, and incoming flows into the system [SOURCE: Page_117]

117 [SOURCE: Page_117]

Chapitre 3 [SOURCE: Page_118]

The profiles of the indices of the scenarios (figure 47) illustrated the main features of each scenario. S$_0$ produced food without mining N from the soil, but lost more NO 3 and had a low NUE. S$_1$ had higher Soil NUE but performed poorly in terms of soil N mining and production, and emitted more pollutants than S$_0$ due to increased NH$_3$ volatilization. S$_2$ was the most efficient in terms of production (i.e., biogas and food) but also the most polluting. S$_3$ had good performances for NUE, environmental losses and biogas production, but at the expense of less food and feed production. [SOURCE: Page_118]

Figure 47. Radar representation of the performance indices of the scenarios. The indices are normalized by their range for the sake of clarity, ie., (max-value)/(max-min) for Natm, Nw, Norg soil, and 1-(max-value)/(max-min) so that values closer to the edge are better. See Table 3 for definitions of the indices. [SOURCE: Page_118]

### 3.4. Discussion

Modeling with TNT$_2$ allowed us to compare a set of realistic changes induced by the introduction of biogas production on a pig farm and predict its long-term effects on NO$_3$ losses at the watershed scale. [SOURCE: Page_118]

118 [SOURCE: Page_118]

Chapitre 3 [SOURCE: Page_119]

Comparing scenarios without changing TNT$_2$'s parameters limited uncertainty in the predictions, but epistemic uncertainties due to the model structure and equations remained (Walker et al., 2003) and could be assessed only by using an ensemble modeling approach (i.e., using different models with the same inputs), which lay beyond the scope of this study. Moreover, the specific soil and climate contexts and characteristics of the simulated farm call for caution when generalizing the conclusions to other locations. Indeed, pig farming systems are very diverse and the purpose of this study was not to represent this diversity. The farm used here is fairly typical for Western Europe, where pig farming is associated with grain production providing part of the feeding of the pigs. Having adopted a process-based, systemic approach provides useful insights into the main factors that influence the environmental impacts of agricultural biogas production, but the magnitude of these impacts will be specific of the context. Therefore, we are confident that the results confirmed the initial hypothesis that the impact of AD on water quality depends mainly on the changes in practices induced by the need to feed the digester (Möller, 2015). Scenarios S$_0$ and S$_1$ had similar predicted NO 3 losses at the outlet, which suggests that replacing slurry with digestate does not increase NO$_3$ leaching. This result is consistent with those of previous studies: when spreading practices are similar and an equivalent amount of effective N is applied, digested pig slurry generally has a risk of leaching similar to that of undigested pig slurry (Möller and Stinner, 2009, Nicholson et al., 2017). Furthermore, replacing CC with ECC, even when fertilized, did not influence leaching greatly when the total amount of N applied per year was similar. It is possible that exporting crop residues rather than incorporating them into the soil compensates for the increased risk of NO 3 leaching due to fertilization. In addition, in S$_1$, fertilization of the main crops was reduced, which can decrease the amount of N left in the soil after harvest (Malone et al., 2018). In comparison, in S$_2$, in which fertilization of the main crops was not reduced, the amount of NO 3 leaching increased, meaning that NO$_3$ leaching tends to increase as the amount by which the regulatory limit is exceeded increases. This highlights the importance of managing the entire crop rotation optimally (Launay et al., 2022), particularly its N fertilization. Specialization and intensification of livestock production systems have changed land use, and the area of permanent grassland has decreased in many regions, either because they are converted to annual crop successions or abandoned and becam fallow land. Given the environmental benefits of permanent grassland, producing energy from its biomass can be one way to reverse this trend (Khalsa et al., 2014). Indeed, feeding grass from grassland into on-farm digesters is expected to become more common in northwestern Europe (McEniry and O'Kiely, 2013). In agreement with many other studies (e.g., Jankowska-Huflejt (2006) and Malik et al. (2022)), introducing extensively managed grassland in S$_3$ decreased NO$_3$ losses effectively. Denitrification was highest in S$_0$ and S$_2$, likely due to the larger total N load to the soil for S$_2$ (Mulvaney et al., 1997) and the plowing of the CC in late winter for S$_0$. The denitrification rate is controlled mainly [SOURCE: Page_119]

119 [SOURCE: Page_119]

Chapitre 3 [SOURCE: Page_120]

by the amount of NO$_3$ in soils in wet conditions (Friedl et al., 2016). Detailed studies of reduction processes in wet soils in Brittany (Jaffrézic, 1997) show that anoxic conditions prevail when the soils are still wet and drainage slows down (i.e., usually in late February-March). Some processes, such as N20 emissions due to nitrification of ammonium applied, are not considered in this version of the model. However, considering that this process represent usually less than 1% of the nitrogen applied (Charles et al., 2017) and that ammonium content in digestate and slurry are similar, this overlooked process is not likely to bias the results. Our results suggest a loss of soil organic matter in the soil under anaerobic digestion scenarios, contrary to several studies (Launay et al., 2022; Levavasseur et al., 2023a). This divergence could be attributed to several factors. First, we used the same decomposition parameters for digestate as for pig slurry, although its residual organic matter is probably more resistant. Second, the maize stalks are not buried but rather exported to the digester, which makes a significant difference in the organic input to the soil. This result should therefore be considered with caution. Field NH$_3$ volatilization depends mainly on the amounts and types of fertilizers applied and the spreading technique (Launay et al., 2022), which are the factors used in TNT$_2$ to estimate it. Fertilization with digestate was assumed to have a higher volatilization rate (S$_1$, S$_2$, and S$_3$) than fertilization with slurry (S$_0$) due to digestate's higher proportion of reduced inorganic N and higher pH (Möller, 2015). Several studies have shown that fertilization with digestates has higher NH$_3$ volatilization than does fertilization with raw livestock waste (Möller and Stinner, 2009; Ni et al., 2012; Crolla et al., 2013; Lili et al., 2016; Nicholson et al., 2017). However, we used relatively low volatilization factors as compared to these references, considering that volatilization could be reduced through phase separation of the digestate (Svehla et al., 2020) and/or less-polluting application techniques, such as injection (Riva et al., 2016, Maris et al., 2021). We chose to limit the changes to a small set of practices to facilitate identification of the main controlling processes. Introducing AD likely induces a wider range of changes, and their effects on farm finances modify the priorities and investment capacities of farmers, leading to more radical changes in the production system. The index profiles highlight the importance of using multiple criteria to assess the performances of a given system. In particular, they show the limits of using NUE as the only criterion: Soil NUE for S$_3$ was highest when including digestate export, but the lowest when considering food and feed production only (Nexpfood/Nin); in contrast, S$_2$ had higher NUE than S$_0$ but emitted much larger amounts of pollutants. [SOURCE: Page_120]

### 3.5. Conclusion and perspectives

Biogas production has become a full-fledged agricultural practice, but its environmental impacts, both positive and negative, are still under debate. The study clearly demonstrated that changes in practices [SOURCE: Page_120]

120 [SOURCE: Page_120]

Chapitre 3 [SOURCE: Page_121]

induced by AD can increase or decrease water quality. The cropping system associated with AD is the most influential factor: the crop rotation and management of fertilization and residues influence the overall sustainability of the system. It is possible to design systems that increase biogas and digestate production while maintaining water quality, but at the expense of food and feed production. The four scenarios were tested through modelling, which enabled exploration of multiple options and increased understanding of the impacts of digester on water quality in a pig farming system. Other performances of the scenarios tested (e.g., other ecosystem services, biotechnical feasibility, profitability) remain to be assessed. Based on this information, policy makers could design measures to promote the most sustainable practices. TNT$_2$ will be used to test other scenarios, including those for dairy or crop farms, with the final aim of upscaling simulations to a larger watershed. Although this study is based on a single situation, the farming system modelled here includes the most typical features of pig breeding farms in the major temperate regions of production: high density of animals, large amount of manure to manage, winter and spring crops in rotation. Consequently, if the magnitudes of fluxes presented here cannot be simply extrapolated, the main conclusions and the approach could be easily transposed to other regions. Indeed, the present study shows that environmental assessment of agricultural AD requires a comprehensive and systemic approach and is an invitation to generalize this type of approach to other contexts. [SOURCE: Page_121]

121 [SOURCE: Page_121]

Chapitre 4. The impacts of biogas production implementation in dairy cattle farms on water quality according to the associated agricultural practices [SOURCE: Page_122]

122 [SOURCE: Page_122]

123 [SOURCE: Page_123]

Chapitre 4 [SOURCE: Page_124]

Title: The impacts of biogas production implementation in dairy cattle farms on water quality according to the associated agricultural practices. Ce chapitre est rédigé sous la forme d’un article scientifique, dans la perspective d’une soumission à une revue pour publication. [SOURCE: Page_124]

Ouarda BAZIZ 1, Fabrice BELINE 1, Patrick DURAND 1 1 INRAE, Institut Agro Rennes-Anger, SAS, 35000 Rennes, France [SOURCE: Page_124]

Résumé Cette étude évalue les impacts potentiels de la digestion anaérobie sur la qualité de l'eau à l'aide d'une approche systémique, en mobilisant le modèle TNT$_2$, capable de simuler les transferts et transformations de l'azote à l'échelle du bassin versant. Cinq scénarios d’élevage laitiers ont été analysés : un scénario de référence sans digestion anaérobie (S$_0$), un scénario avec digestion anaérobie et changements minimaux (S$_1$), un scénario combinant digestion anaérobie et robot de traite (S$_2$), un scénario associant digestion anaérobie et réduction du cheptel (S$_3$), et enfin, un scénario agroécologique (S$_4$). Les résultats révèlent que le scénario intensif S$_2$ est le plus performant en termes de production de biogaz et de rendement alimentaire, mais il génère également les impacts les plus négatifs sur la qualité de l'eau. Le scénario S$_3$, maximise la production de biogaz mais tend à augmenter les risques de pollution par rapport à S$_0$ et présente un compromis avec une moindre rentabilité alimentaire. En revanche, le scénario extensif S$_4$ se distingue comme le moins polluant, tout en maintenant une production de biogaz significative. Cette étude illustre l'intérêt d'une approche systémique, en montrant que l'impact de la méthanisation dépend fortement des pratiques agricoles mises en œuvre. Elle souligne également l'impossibilité de répondre simultanément à tous les enjeux, exigeant ainsi des arbitrages entre objectifs environnementaux, énergétiques et alimentaires. [SOURCE: Page_124]

Abstract [SOURCE: Page_124]

This study evaluates the potential impacts of anaerobic digestion on water quality using a systemic approach, leveraging the TNT$_2$ model, which is capable of simulating nitrogen transfers and transformations at the watershed scale. Five scenarios were analysed: a reference scenario without anaerobic digestion (S$_0$), a scenario with anaerobic digestion and minimal changes (S$_1$), a scenario with anaerobic digestion with milking robot (S$_2$), a scenario with anaerobic digestion and decrease of livestock (S$_3$), and finally, an agroecological scenario (S$_4$). The results reveal that the intensive S$_2$ scenario is the most efficient in terms of biogas production and food yield but also generates the most negative impacts on water quality. The S$_3$ scenario maximize biogas production but increase impacts on water quality as regards to S$_0$, presenting a trade-off with [SOURCE: Page_124]

124 [SOURCE: Page_124]

Chapitre 4 [SOURCE: Page_125]

lower food production profitability. In contrast, the extensive S$_4$ scenario stands out as the least polluting while maintaining significant biogas production. This study highlights the value of a systemic approach, showing that the impact of anaerobic digestion strongly depends on the agricultural practices implemented. It also emphasizes the impossibility of simultaneously addressing all challenges, thus requiring trade-offs between environmental, energy, and food objectives. [SOURCE: Page_125]

### 4.1. Introduction

Agriculture is constantly evolving and must adopt sustainable practices to meet the growing needs of our society while preserving our environment. Anaerobic digestion (AD) is one of the changes widely adopted by farmers (Vaneeckhaute et al., 2013). This technology, which involves valorizing organic waste by producing biogas, arouses keen interest as a source of renewable energy while could enhance circularity. In France, biogas accounts for approximately 5% of the renewable energy mix and this share is steadily growing. France is the leading producer of beef in Europe and a major dairy producer (Farm Europe, 2019). Cattle farming generates substantial quantities of organic waste, including manure, slurry and enteric fermentation, which pose significant environmental management challenges, as methane emissions represent 46% of the 80.9 Mt CO$_2$-eq emitted per year (CITEPA, 2022). Ruminant farming accounts for 10.4% of global emissions (IDELE, 2015). These wastes are rich in nutrients such as nitrogen (N) and phosphorus (P), which, if not properly managed, can lead to the contamination of water bodies through leaching and runoff. Through the process of anaerobic digestion, organic waste is transformed into biogas and a residual product known as digestate. This digestate contains a large portion of the nutrients originally present in the organic matter, and is generally used as a fertiliser, considered as more easily manageable than cattle waste. This is why there is a strong synergy between anaerobic digestion and cattle farming. Beyond this energy production and the transformation of waste in a new fertilizer, anaerobic digestion brings about changes in the agricultural system once integrated (Möller, 2015). This includes the introduction of new crops in the field rotations to feed the digester or changes in the proportions of crops, changes in the management of the cattle and its feeding, and many more options. Some of them may result more input and more production, others will have the opposite consequence. Some prospective studies (e.g. Afterres 2050, 2022) see biogas production as an opportunity of maintaining grasslands in a context of overall decrease of livestock in Europe, while others highlights the risk on water quality of increased silage maize cropping. This diversity of changes, depending on the choices of the farmers and on the structure of the farms, makes it difficult to draw conclusions on the environmental impacts of biogas production and calls for a systemic approach of the question. [SOURCE: Page_125]

125 [SOURCE: Page_125]

Chapitre 4 [SOURCE: Page_126]

However, a majority of studies so far at the local level have focussed on the consequences of substituting mineral fertilizers, manure and slurry by digestates, and this is already a complex matter. Indeed, this depends on various factors, including the composition of the digestate, the timing and method of its application, and the characteristics of the local environment. Studies (Pötsch, 2004; Möller et Stinner 2009; Svoboda et al. 2013; Nicholson et al., 2017) have shown that the risk of nitrate (NO₃⁻) leaching from fields fertilized with digestate can be comparable to that from fields fertilized with untreated manure or synthetic fertilizers. The aim of this study is to envision the question of the consequences of AD adoption by dairy farmers on nitrate pollution of surface water in a broader perspective, by taking into account a diversity of changes that can occur on the farm due to AD implementation, and by looking at the integrated effects at the whole farm scale. The approach used was (1) To construct at farm level contrasting scenarios based on possible changes caused by the introduction of AD in a cattle farm, (2) To simulate N these scenario using the agro-hydrological model TNT$_2$ (Beaujouan et al., 2002) coupled with simple models of AD and cattle. Finally, the simulations provided an assessment of the changes in nitrogen flows in the different component of the system, allowing a multicriteria evaluation of the scenarios. [SOURCE: Page_126]

### 4.2.1. Study area and experiment design

For the sake of flexibility and simplicity of the scenarios’ construction and assessment, we chose to simulate a virtual farm whose fields would cover entirely the area of a small catchment. To make it as realistic and representative as possible, we chose to locate it in a subcatchment which is part of a well- known study site, and to give it the average characteristics of the surrounding dairy farms. The same approach was used in a previous study (Baziz et al., 2024). The 104 ha subcatchment chosen, Keramiot, is part of the Kervidy-Naizin catchment located in the nothern part (Figure 48), a 4.9 km² second-order catchment situated in central Brittany, France. It is the main study site of ORE AgrHys (Agronomy and Hydrology Research Observatory), and, as such, has been extensively monitored for more than 30 years to study the impacts of land use and climate change and on hydrological systems. Agriculture predominantly characterizes the land use within this watershed, with a mix of arable farming and livestock breeding. This results in a mosaic of variety of crops (such as cereals, maize, leys, grasslands, and vegetables). The major part of this production is for feeding the livestock bred in the farms (including dairy and meat bovines, pigs, and poultry). The climate is a mild oceanic climate (mean annual temperature of 11.2 °C and mean rainfall of 827 mm) while the bedrock is schists overlaid by moderately acidic brown soils on gentle slopes (elevation between 98 and 140 m. a.s.l.). See Gascuel- Odoux et al. (2018) for more information of the Kervidy-Naizin catchment. [SOURCE: Page_126]

126 [SOURCE: Page_126]

Chapitre 4 [SOURCE: Page_127]

The observations from this catchment inspired the development of the TNT$_2$ model that has been extensively tested using the detailed data on soil, discharge, concentrations and agriculture practices available (see Casal et al., 2019 for the more recent one). The Kéramiot sub-catchment is a 1st order catchment, where, in the real world several farms (dairy, poultry and pig farms) operate 101 ha of the total 104 ha, the remaining being farmsteads, roads and small woodlots. The stream is not monitored regularly, but occasional observations suggest that is has similar chemical and hydrological functioning as the main catchment, and therefore we used for the model the same parameter sets as the main catchment (Casal et al., 2019). Since the aim of the study was to compare virtual situations, a more precise parameterisation is not really an issue here. For this study, a fictitious dairy farm based on real soil, climate and farming system characteristics was created to operate on the 101 ha of Keramiot’s agricultural land. The farm cattle breed a herd of 77 dairy cows and 58 heifers, corresponding to the average LSU/ha (livestock unit density) for the region (Agreste, 2023), and the crop system is typical of the dairy farms of the area, i.e., a mix of permanent grassland, ley and silage maize to feed the animal, and some cash crops (winter cereals). The study focused on modelling nitrogen flows at the catchment scale by considering the interactions between soil, plants, and hydrological dynamics. The simulation utilized climatic data recorded from 1995 to 2020 in the Agrhys observatory. [SOURCE: Page_127]

Figure 48. The Kervidy Naizin watershed and the Keramiot sub-watershed [SOURCE: Page_127]

127 [SOURCE: Page_127]

Chapitre 4 [SOURCE: Page_128]

4.2.2. Soil and groundwater nitrogen fluxes modelling by the tool Topography Nitrogen Transfer and Transformations (TNT$_2$) [SOURCE: Page_128]

The modeling tool used in this study for agro-hydrological system is TNT$_2$. This model is designed to simulate discharge and nitrate concentration at the outlet with climate and agricultural operations as forcing variables. TNT$_2$ is based on the coupling of a hydrological model inspired by TOPMODEL (Beven, 1997) and the crop model STICS (Brisson et al., 1998). It is a spatially distributed model utilizing a Digital Elevation Model (DEM) (Morales and Marín, 2016), which means that the calculations are made at the individual cell level. The model is based on concepts from TOPMODEL, a theoretical framework that simplifies the understanding of water flows according to topography. It is based on the hypothesis that the discharge at the catchment outlet is primarily fed by dynamics of a shallow groundwater that can generate surface runoff in downslopes areas where the soil is saturated during rainfall events (Neto et al., 2019). The model stands out for its ability to simulate water flows while accounting for topographical specificities and the spatial dynamics of the environment. This model is particularly suited to regions where shallow aquifers strongly influence hydrology, such as in certain parts of Western Europe (Ferrant et al., 2011). It evaluates overland flux based on the saturated area of the soil (Xue et al., 2018) and uses a darcyan formalism to simulate water transfer through the subsoil, considering a hydraulic gradient proportional to the terrain slope and a transmissivity that decreases with soil depth. It divides each watershed cell into layers to differentiate between mobile and immobile water, with vertical flows in the unsaturated zone and lateral flows in the saturated zone (Beaujouan et al., 2002). The TNT$_2$ model requires spatially distributed information on agricultural practices as input, such as crop rotations and chronological successions of technical operations (sowing, fertilization, harvest). This information is used as an input to a simplified version of STICS, developed by INRAE in France, which is widely used to analyze and predict crop growth, development, and yield under various environmental conditions. It operates as a dynamic model, simulating the daily response of crops to weather conditions and agricultural operations (Brisson et al., 2003). STICS integrates the carbon, nitrogen, water, and energy cycles within the soil-crop system. The model is capable of simulating a wide range of crops (cereals, legumes, vegetables, etc.), making it a versatile tool for agronomic studies. It is customizable and can be adjusted to specific conditions (Di Bella et al., 2005). It has been simplified and integrated into TNT$_2$ to comprehensively model hydrological and agricultural interactions by entirely rewriting the model in C++. The study aimed to examine the impact of practice changes on water quality following the implementation of an AD unit. Together with literature and expert knowledge compilation, a survey was conducted among cattle farmers having experimented such installation to gather insights on the possible changes. [SOURCE: Page_128]

128 [SOURCE: Page_128]

Chapitre 4 [SOURCE: Page_129]

### 4.2.3. Modelling of nitrogen fluxes from animals and the effluents management

As already mentioned, the number of dairy cows (77) was calculated using the average ratio of 0.75 head/ha of the region. Regarding the heifers, we calculated the average ratio of replacement heifers (less than 1y. old, 1-2y old and more than 2y.) based on data from CITEPA (2023). After calculating the number of dairy cows and heifers, we used the reference data from Légifrance (2023) to estimate the nitrogen available for crop fertilisation for each category (table 14). The data from this reference issued from French regulations corresponds to nitrogen returning to soils, i.e (N Pasture + N Spread) on figure 49. So, the nitrogen returning to soils on the farm was calculated by multiplying the [SOURCE: Page_129]

amount of nitrogen available for crop fertilization per animal by the total number of animals present on the farm. The values from table 14 also allow us to calculate the ratio between the excretion during grazing (EG) and the excretion in the builds, that will have to be managed (EI), by supposing proportionality between the time spent indoor or outdoor and the amount of excretion. On the other hands, for the amount of nitrogen produced per dairy cow, we assumed that each cow produces 8000 kg of milk per year. Considering a percentage of 0.54% nitrogen in the milk exported and nitrogen in the meat produced, we calculated the amount of N dairy +meat produced. For calculation of nitrogen in the meat, another factor taken into account is the replacement rate of dairy cows, which represents the percentage of cows in a herd that are replaced each year by new heifers. Generally, this rate is often between 25% and 35% per year. In this study, a factor of 33% was used (Lengagne, 2019). The live weight of the dairy cows was estimated at 650 kg (Chambre d’agriculture Bretagne, 2005). To calculate the nitrogen equivalent based on live weight, a value of 0.024 kg N/kg of live weight for a dairy cow was used (Espagnol et al., 2022). Then, a Nitrogen Conversion Efficiency (NCE) factor of 0.22 was used in the study corresponding to data from Rouillé et al. (2023) for farm with more than 30% of maize in the forage aera. This factor corresponds to the efficiency with which cows convert the nitrogen they ingest through their feed into milk and meat, and it is a measure of bovine productivity. Such factor allows us to calculate N feed and N excreted from N dairy +meat. The difference between N Building and N Spread was considered as N Losses building + storage, [SOURCE: Page_129]

nitrogen lost through volatilization during storage and in the building (figure 49). The N feed farm was calculated using the TNT$_2$ model, and the N purch feed was determined by subtracting the N feed farm from the N feed requirements. [SOURCE: Page_129]

129 [SOURCE: Page_129]

Chapitre 4 [SOURCE: Page_130]

Figure 49. Nitrogen fluxes from animals. [SOURCE: Page_130]

(N purch feed= nitrogen from feed purchased, N feed farm: nitrogen from feed produced on farm, N diary + meat: Nitrogen into diary milk and meat, N excreted: nitrogen excreted by cows, N pasture: nitrogen excreted at pasture, N building: nitrogen excreted into the building, N losses building + storage: nitrogen losses from building and storage, N spread: remaining nitrogen for spreading). [SOURCE: Page_130]

The excretions of dairy cows are considered as slurry, while those of heifers are treated as manure, as is the case in many farms in the region. [SOURCE: Page_130]

The quantities of maize, green rye, and grass were estimated based on simulation results obtained from the agro-environmental STICS model. For forage crops, such as maize and grass, a two-step methodology was adopted. First, the quantities produced were integrated into a forage balance to meet the animals' nutritional needs. Then, any production surpluses, identified after satisfying forage requirements, were considered available for feeding the biogas unit. The volumes of slurry and manure produced on the farm were calculated using the nitrogen flows previously estimated (based on the calculated animal excretions) and the nitrogen concentrations provided in Table 15. This approach links nitrogen flows to the quantities of effluents produced, taking into account the specific characteristics of the different types of animal excretions. [SOURCE: Page_131]

### 4.2.5. Construction of scenarios

Five scenarios were developed to explore the diversity in cattle farms. It should be noted that the farm area remains constant across all scenarios. 4.2.5.1. Baseline scenario without anaerobic digestion (S$_0$) [SOURCE: Page_131]

The reference scenario (figure 50) is based on current data. The crop rotation includes 1/3 maize silage, 1/4 winter cereals (including winter wheat and barley), 1/7 permanent grassland, and 1/4 temporary grassland. These proportions reflect the average crop rotation in Brittany according to Agreste (2023). These crops are fertilized with cattle slurry and manure, complying with the limit of 170 Kg N/ha, and with mineral fertilizers, not exceeding a total fertilization limit of 210 Kg N/ha. Nitrate catch crops are also sown between the main crops and are buried at the end of their cycle. The ammonia emission factors considered for cattle slurry and cattle manure are 18% and 10% of NH$_4$ content, respectively, when buried immediately after application, and 21% and 30%, respectively, when not buried. [SOURCE: Page_131]

### 4.2.5.2. Scenario with anaerobic digestion with minimal changes (S$_1$)

In this scenario (figure 51), an AD unit has been implemented. The crop rotation has not been changed, except that the catch crops (CC) have been replaced by Energy Cover Crops (ECC) where possible. The ECC were fertilized with 100 Kg N/ha of digestate in February. Rye was used as the ECC crop. The main crops were fertilized with raw digestate and mineral fertilizers. The emission factor for digestate when it is buried just after application is 18% for all scenarios that include AD and 29% when it is non- buried. [SOURCE: Page_132]

### 4.2.5.3. Scenario with anaerobic digestion with milking robot (S$_2$)

In this scenario (figure 52), increased financial capability due to AD led to the purchase of a milking robot on the farm. This device is used in the dairy industry to automate the milking process of dairy cows. This usually lead to the dairy cows being either indoors or in grasslands neighbouring the farmstead, to allow them to access freely to the robot. Therefore, the main change in the crop rotation concerned the management practices of temporary and permanent grasslands. The accessible permanent grasslands (13.7 % of the area) are exclusively grazed and temporary grasslands are exclusively mowed (to feed the animals indoors). Milk production is increased to 10,000 litres per cow and year due to the increase of concentrates and the individualization of the diet adjustment for the dairy cows, and as a result, N excretion also increased to 111 kg per animal/year (Légifrance, 2023) even if an increase of NCE was considered from 0.22 to 0.23. [SOURCE: Page_133]

### 4.2.5.4. Scenario with anaerobic digestion and decrease of livestock (S$_3$)

The introduction of AD may encourage some farmers to reduce their livestock and increase their biogas production, because of more stable prices and less work. This scenario (figure 53) aimed at testing this option in the more favourable case for water quality, i.e by maintaining the same proportions of crops, the excess of grass and maize production being fed into the digester. The number of dairy cows was reduced by approximately 40%. As in S$_1$, the catch crops (CC) have been replaced with rye (ECC) where possible, with minimal changes in crop rotation. All data concerning animal and manure/slurry were similar to S$_0$ and S$_1$. [SOURCE: Page_133]

### 4.2.5.5. Agroecological scenario S$_4$

According to Gliessman (2018), agroecology is currently undergoing significant evolution and is gaining ground as a viable solution to the environmental and social challenges associated with agriculture. It is especially recognized for its potential to sustainably transform food systems. Some dairy systems in Brittany qualified as agroecological are based on permanent grasslands only, with less productive cows but much less expenses. Here we have imagined such a scenario (figure 54), given that keeping constant the size of the cattle and devoting all the area to grasslands would allow the farmer to use a significant portion of the grass production to feed the digester. [SOURCE: Page_134]

Figure 54. Nitrogen flows considered in scenario S$_4$ [SOURCE: Page_134]

134 [SOURCE: Page_134]

Chapitre 4 [SOURCE: Page_135]

As a result, since the animals are fed only grass, nitrogen excretion per dairy cow have been increased by about 10%, and milk production is 5500 liters/year (Giovanni and Dulphy, 2008). Permanent grasslands are fertilized and alternated between cup and grazing. The excess production is used for biogas production. All the simulated scenarios are summarized in table 16 for animal characteristics and in table 17 for crops. [SOURCE: Page_135]

### 4.3.1. Impacts of scenarios on water quality at the outlet of the catchment

The figure 55 shows the evolution of accumulated nitrogen flux (in kg/ha) at the outlet of the catchment over 25 years of simulation for the different scenarios. We observe that this flux, i.e the losses in scenarios S$_1$ and S$_3$ are 12.7 % and 14.1 % higher, respectively, than those in S$_0$, while those in S$_2$ are 28 % higher. Scenario S$_4$, on the other hand, is 27.5% lower than the reference situation (S$_0$). [SOURCE: Page_136]
